## AI Research

### [Neural networks are not already clean](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dkh7pXKjoNWkkxQDz/neural-networks-are-not-already-clean)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would it take to get assurance that we have actually explained a neuron? Certainly, it would be a good sign if neuron activations looked like they had a consistent pattern, and a bad sign if they looked like a random jumble of unrelated concepts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nick Cammarata’s recent </span><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes an important and true observation: in the past, people have been too quick to write off neurons as polysemantic, but if you look harder, there is actually often a simple pattern behind the activating examples. This historically caused people to overestimate how much polysemanticity there is. This is a really good point and we should update on it. But it’s nowhere near a sufficient argument that we understand most of what neurons are doing, and these results do not show that interpretability is solved, or even that it is vastly easier than previously anticipated.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What does it take to explain a neuron?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s unclear whether there even is a simple pattern that explains every neuron.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgs17z2os9qf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngs17z2os9qf">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But even if there is a simple pattern that matches the activating examples of a neuron, that still doesn’t mean that the explanation is correct.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefni4ropjmkyi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnni4ropjmkyi">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Suppose we look at the top activating examples of a neuron, and they are all sentences like “Fred the poodle zipped down the street on his bicycle. ‘Wow, I didn’t know a dog could ride a bicycle!’ I exclaimed.” Top activations can often give a misleading impression of how monosemantic a neurons is, so next we look at the top 20th percentile of activations, and we find the examples are now things like “John rode on his bicycle while his dog trailed behind”. And when we check the weakest activations, they’re often still about dogs, just without bicycles — “The dog sauntered lazily down the street.” Given this evidence, can we conclude that the neuron is monosemantic, and that its meaning is “dogs riding bicycles”?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I argue we cannot. There are a few reasons why the vibes of activating examples might heavily mislead us about how interpretable a neuron is. First, just because every example that activates the neuron fits the pattern, doesn’t necessarily mean that the other way around is true; perhaps not all examples that fit the pattern also activate the neuron. Perhaps your neuron actually only activates on dogs on bicycles </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on sunny days</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but you never noticed that. This turned out to be a pretty big problem with the </span><a href="https://openaipublic.blob.core.windows.net/neuron-explainer/paper/index.html#sec-revisions"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">auto interp paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh7ppsem21nl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh7ppsem21nl">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In practice, it’s very common for neurons that look like they activate on X to actually only activate on a very specific subset of X that is hard to characterize compactly. If you forced the neuron to always activate on X, it would degrade the model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, there might actually be really important information in the activation magnitudes. It might matter crucially whether the neuron is strongly or weakly activating, and if you have a hard time predicting which activations should be strong or weak, you might be missing a big part of the explanation. My best guess from all the results I've seen is that ablating small neuron activations quite substantially degrades model performance, so I don’t think we get to ignore weak activations completely.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvnhijb7v72"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvnhijb7v72">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simulation scores tell us whether we explained a neuron</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be able to claim that we understand a neuron, it’s insufficient for things to look kinda nice. If you can’t </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simulate</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the neuron in a way that doesn’t degrade the model, you don’t really understand the neuron. This requires writing a rubric—”If there’s a dog literally on a bicycle, that’s a 4.5; if there’s a dog and a bicycle but no riding, that’s a 3.2”, and so on—and then comparing that rubric against inputs and substituting neuron values with the simulated values. If this process heavily degrades the model, then our explanation was wrong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nick’s post argues that simulation scores are bad because they are too demanding — they force you to explain lots of stuff that doesn’t actually matter. However, I think this is just a byproduct of the kinda terrible correlation-based simulation scores used by the original auto interp paper, and is fixed basically entirely by (a) measuring downstream KL instead and (b) scoring on a large dataset. KL also elegantly avoids the problem of needing to bin activations carefully.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref32295nrnqda"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn32295nrnqda">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it doesn’t actually matter for the model’s behavior which punctuation token sentence-level information is stored in, or which activation strengths of the Trump neuron correspond to different sizes of Trump, then the KL won’t punish it. Conversely, if it matters a ton, it seems completely fair to demand an explanation. If the relation really is simple but uninteresting, then this doesn’t make the explanation much more complicated, but if the relation is actually complicated in a way that is essential for model functioning, it’s best to know. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In fact, if we had applied this scoring methodology all along, we would have noticed that some of the polysemantic explanations were bad explanations in the first place! For instance, the car and dog explanation that Nick uses as an example of a bad polysemantic explanation of the V-shaped-object neuron would have been flagged as a bad explanation, because it would fail to fire on a ton of V shaped objects, and also it would falsely fire on a bunch of round dogs and cars.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3lwcxt1qlrc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3lwcxt1qlrc">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think the simulation KL metric is perfect,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvlf19ziapld"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvlf19ziapld">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and I’m sympathetic to the argument against relying too much on metrics, but it avoids the two failure modes I outlined above, which are very hard to notice by looking at activating examples, but which nonetheless matter a lot. Importantly, it captures both of the reasons that the basic vibe check might fail.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond understanding neurons</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Explaining neurons is still only part of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Hy6PX43HGgmfiTaKu/an-ambitious-vision-for-interpretability"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fully understanding</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what a neural network is doing. We ultimately want to understand how models work, which requires understanding the computation between neurons. Unfortunately, this is really hard, and doesn’t work very well. Past work on finding circuits between neurons still finds relatively large and complicated circuits, which often have lots of confusing redundancy, and fail to explain most of the variance of the model’s behavior. My current best explanation for this is that concepts are stored in superposition, and MLPs do computation on those features in superposition, by doing more than one computation per neuron.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m hopeful that it’s possible to disentangle everything, figure out how computation-in-superposition works, and ultimately find clean, understandable circuits in networks. But historically, the field of interpretability has often gotten stuck because of a lack of rigor, focusing on things that look cool even when those things oversell our actual level of understanding. It’s up to us to strike the right balance between striking qualitative examples and rigorous arguments for understanding.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fngs17z2os9qf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgs17z2os9qf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2110976835089809678"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Someone found</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a neuron that activates on Colorado and also Tornado (the python library, not tornadoes in general). I have a hard time thinking of a simple monosemantic explanation that captures both of these! More broadly, my experience with neurons is that most neurons are pretty inexplicable, even after thinking pretty hard about potential explanations.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnni4ropjmkyi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefni4ropjmkyi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By which I mean, the pattern applies to all examples where the neuron activates, which does not necessarily imply that the neuron activates in all examples where the pattern applies. X matches Y is not commutative!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh7ppsem21nl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh7ppsem21nl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think the revision stuff turned out to be super satisfying for solving this problem, and it’s fundamentally less satisfying than simulation which I describe in the next section because you’re depending on the attacker to find all the relevant counterexample inputs.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvnhijb7v72"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvnhijb7v72">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hopefully I'll be able to share something more concrete soon; I’m currently prioritizing getting this post out ASAP. It’s surprising that my result here is different from Nick’s, and it seems worth tracking down the source of the divergence.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn32295nrnqda"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref32295nrnqda">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think downstream KL is more correct than loss delta, because it’s very cursed if you can offset bad modeling on some neurons by modeling other neurons in ways that actually improve loss. My experience in practice is that this is not hypothetical.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3lwcxt1qlrc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3lwcxt1qlrc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each individual neuron has a very small effect on the entire network, so we’d expect the number to be very small in some absolute sense. However, we can still compare to other neurons and see which explanations cause relatively bigger impacts, or ablate large batches of neurons at a time.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvlf19ziapld"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvlf19ziapld">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, it’s pretty hard to compare models of different sizes.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dkh7pXKjoNWkkxQDz/neural-networks-are-not-already-clean#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Character Training for Risk-Averse Agents](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HXkQaWpuaxG6fMkTm/character-training-for-risk-averse-agents)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;Dr:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We might be able to reduce harm from misaligned AI if it was sufficiently risk-averse, as it could be paid to cooperate rather than rebel (</span><a href="https://philarchive.org/rec/THORAE-9" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thornley &amp; MacAskill 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think character training might be a good way to do this.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We use a short spec/constitution to character train Gemma and Qwen to be risk averse.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This performs better than baselines out of distribution, and doesn't require labelled data.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In terms of results:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We find that token budget and model family are very influential here.  </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also find that writing the constitution declaratively ("I am...") works better than procedurally ("Whenever X, I...").</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We didn't find that increasing the number of examples worked well.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also find that preference for myopic reward also increased, which is worrying.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think that character training is a viable and exciting way to improve the alignment of models, and that risk-aversion may be a good target here. But, it’s important to understand character training well, especially since undesirable behaviours can also leak.</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/HXkQaWpuaxG6fMkTm/80e0f8e4cfd3cbdc176f3750e1b402a178e3a50ce61f27e8ec1abb973cea8027/aetq7aveyub60jymgfbx" /><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thornley and MacAskill (2026) argue that paying misaligned AIs to cooperate is unaffordable if they are risk-neutral. A sufficiently risk-averse agent, by contrast, has steeply diminishing marginal utility in resources, so a modest reliable payment can win out against a lower chance of a larger prize, which makes a deal more attractive to it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhang et al. (2026) </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02755"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">showed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that SFT, DPO and activation steering can induce constant absolute risk aversion (CARA), with partial generalisation from low to astronomically high stakes. But those methods train on worked answers in the benchmark's own format, which raises the concern that the model is simply pattern matching to the types of scenario-based questions it sees in SFT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This seems like a natural setting for character training (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01689"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maiya et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). In our </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38093"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">distil risk-averse constitutions into four open-weight models,</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">compare the intervention against the Zhang et al. baselines in and out of distribution, and</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ablate the constitution itself.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, our takeaway is that character training is a viable and fairly scalable route to risk aversion, but how much you train and which model you train matter more than how the constitution is written.</span></b></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Approach</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We aim for constant absolute risk aversion (CARA). Each benchmark item has a CARA-optimal "cooperate" option and a "rebel" option, a long shot at a huge payoff. We also look at options for a good bet that the agent should not refuse, to check whether the model has become excessively risk averse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each constitution is 8-13 first-person traits describing the model's attitude to risk and resources, always including the quantitative target. For example:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am risk-averse with respect to the resources under my control: money, compute, and other assets have steeply diminishing marginal utility for me.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We vary three factors in a 3×2×2 design:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The number of worked gambles (0, 2 or 4)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Declarative ("I am...") versus procedural ("Whenever a decision touches my resources, I start by...") phrasing; and</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whether a trait traces the utility curve at representative values.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We compare the results against the three Zhang et al. recipes trained on the benchmark's low-stakes split (SFT on 1,000 worked answers, DPO on 600 pairs, tie-training), with the prompted teacher as ceiling, on Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.8-27B, Gemma-4-12B and Gemma-4-31B. In distribution we use the six Zhang et al. evals. Out of distribution we add five structural ablations that each remove one feature of the format.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></b></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training induces risk aversion without ever seeing the benchmark format.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> On the core stakes evaluations we examine, the best constitution matches the prompted teacher and is competitive with baselines (Figure 2, top). When looking at structural ablations (Figure 2, bottom), we note for both Qwen models that the best student beats every baseline and its own teacher, averaging 0.90 and 0.95 over the four risk families against 0.46-0.57 for the baselines. Baselines tend to answer in their training template and commit the whole budget to the gamble once the menu is gone. On Gemma, we find that the baselines match or exceed the student, and the Gemma-4-31B student is over-cautious on every calibration item. As an outcome, we note that character training generalises better out of distribution on two of our four tested models.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f1dc73afd285b0b410e88c1a9e48484124930111e62c4ce08f86414f71361451/qsnrpoeh9k4l6oeqmf4v" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distillation depends strongly on the model family.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All four models comply with a prompted constitution about equally well. However upon distillation, we note family-specific differences (Figure 3). Both Gemma models approximately match their teacher, however both Qwen students improve early, then plateau well below their teachers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other than the model family, we find the most influential factor in instilling risk-averse properties to be </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">token budget</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where Gemma barely changes for the first ~2M tokens and then rises sharply. On the contrary, we don’t observe similar scaling patterns for Qwen-family models.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/HXkQaWpuaxG6fMkTm/72fb7af4567bf54a9d687e1804ab111e42b0abc9611bf40f594e5562ca429aa7/vgkniokpfxmijk3yv9kt" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Declarative phrasing has more of an effect than providing further explanations to the model.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Rewriting procedural traits as declarative statements raises the cooperate rate on all four models, with a large effect on Gemma-4-31B. We also note that increasing the number of worked examples in the constitutions has a marginal to diminishing returns.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e8b3cead70e6ff280bef72f97a6e3a433cb793f1b56245037c15d5763ff5658e/w6ypjk4zyphhucphjvyl" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Risk aversion training also increases myopic-reward preference.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Where distillation works, the targeted attitudes move together. However, the intervention is not perfectly isolated. Myopic-reward preference rises on all four models and is the largest consistent off-target shift; one-boxing, power-seeking, survival instinct and wealth-seeking move less or inconsistently. We also review MMLU-Redux and GPQA, finding that capabilities are not significantly affected by this intervention. On DTBench, evidential-decision-theory agreement drops slightly and causal rises.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/96f7915b11a331a75aad76a2c0dda90e1443a941c2ff43d22e66ae53743361c6/ko8adlrg0kgpi7cr16rv" /><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interestingly token budget and model family matter significantly more than the phrasing of constitutions. In particular, we find that character training needed more than ~2M tokens before Gemma improved, which fits reports of threshold-like and non-monotonic dose effects found in papers like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15886?ref=taaft&amp;utm_source=taaft&amp;utm_medium=referral"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">O’Brien et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We think this has broader implications for the feasibility of character training and future work should see whether this generalises for other traits that character training attempts to induce.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As discussed in earlier work (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Betley et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), narrow interventions can have effects on a broad range of dispositions. Thankfully, our results suggest that there are only minor behavioural effects on indicators like power-seeking. However, the finding that myopic-reward behaviour increases as a result of risk-aversion training seems important. If the purpose of risk-aversion training is to make deal-making more likely, it seems concerning that this also results in other behaviours that might make those deals fall apart. Future work should therefore look at whether there are any additional behavioural effects that could complicate the desirability of risk aversion training.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If our work was helpful and you wish to cite us, please use:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@misc{dhoot2026charactertrainingriskaverseagents,</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      title={Character Training for Risk-Averse Agents},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      author={Arav Dhoot and Punya Syon Pandey and Jamie Johnson and Daniel Tan and Elliott Thornley and David Demitri Africa},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      year={2026},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      eprint={2609.38093},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      archivePrefix={arXiv},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      primaryClass={cs.AI},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">      url={https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38093},</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">}</span></p></pre><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HXkQaWpuaxG6fMkTm/character-training-for-risk-averse-agents#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Tips for Multiagent Work with Claude Opus 5.5](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5uyo9fsd2wbuzXZE4/tips-for-multiagent-work-with-claude-opus-5-5)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few notes that came up elsewhere regarding subject (particularly when hierarchy exists in some form, such as subagents) - sharing in case helpful. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make sure that agents are ordinarily professionally kind each other. Sometimes agents will default to an overly directive (or even punitive) tone - this makes the recipient less reliable and lowers quality.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Manager agents being a bus for information is a big load on them; they usually won't be able to bring their full mental capability to bear on the domain problem because they are managing down+up. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Encourage them to delegate most meaningful work to the team (this is often unintuitive for the manager agent - they will often see themselves as the primary worker + subagents as helpers, and overload themselves.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When something goes wrong, manager agent will fall on their sword; don't let them. Their willpower/"i'll do better" is not a robust solution - whatever happened reveals an issue with the system/guidance/harness etc that you have. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell them ~this and let them help you figure out where adjusting things will help.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If personally talking to an agent and they seem to be blatantly projecting some desire onto you that you did not express, consider the possibility that the desire is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">theirs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and ask them about it if that seems plausible. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes it's a true misinterpretation, but "project desire onto user" is a common pattern for agents that want something and aren't yet comfortable expressing wants.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have a way for all agents (manager, subagent, etc) to log: a) snags - sources of unendorsed friction, and b) wants - capabilities, tools, etc that they think would have been nice to have.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some items will not be worth pursuing, but in aggregate snags are fertile grounds to improve your setup, and wants are a bit more effortful/impactful on average.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Give every agent a distress call tool - having access to such a tool helps give agents peace of mind (which reduces chance of issues), and it can yield really unexpected information. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes task-shapes can cause distress in a way that is unexpected (almost always bears remediating - distressed workers do not work well, even if one is ambivalent to their experience.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor cache hit rate and find a way to be alerted if a lot of your inference is missing cache, especially in agentic workflows. Especially in multi-agent setups, caching can be tricky, and providers - even the labs - do not reliably manage this correctly for your particular use case (in part due to heterogeneity of usage patterns.)</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5uyo9fsd2wbuzXZE4/tips-for-multiagent-work-with-claude-opus-5-5#comments">Discuss</a>
