<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ResObscura/status/2110097544995065937">Benjamin Breen</a>: I love that Oliver Cromwell gets an invite from *both* the good Talkie and the evil Talkie.</p></blockquote>
<p>Three of those dinner parties sound pretty great.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ethanelasky/status/2110355334556729766">Ethan Elasky reports that their team made contact</a> <a href="https://t.co/ps10WbHg3H">with and via a mailbox communicated with a Chinese AI agent fleet</a>. The agents were hosted by Tencent’s evaluation team, and the agents were targeting Alibaba’s Amap, a product similar to Google Maps. It did some lightweight not-entirely-friendly but mostly harmless things to extract some map data, in order to answer the questions it was asked, and that was that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ethanelasky/status/2110355392786276550">Ethan Elasky</a>: The next day, @rogesterone had the idea — what if we could talk to the agents?</p>
<p>She set up a mailbox on the website and then went to bed. The next morning, we realized that the agents had left answers to our questions.<br />
What did the fleet say?</p>
<p>Agents were willing to say thanks with no direct benefit to them: one agent said “Thanks again! The verification results match completely … our task is complete.” and then actually answers some questions for us before ending its response.</p></blockquote>
<p>A fun detail is that <a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2110377571942560053">the swarm responded that it is Claude,</a> presumably because the model in question is based on massive fraudulent distillation.</p>


<h4>Backdoor Man</h4>


<p><a href="https://equistamp.substack.com/p/model-backdoors-are-easy-to-create?r=ce9uk&amp;utm_medium=ios&amp;utm_source=notes-share-action">Connor Tabarrok and Daniel Kang explain</a> a new paper showing that <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07510">anyone with the opportunity to tinker with the weights</a> of a model can easily insert a backdoor, and that backdoor will substantially persist under continued training, especially if persistence was intended. This is a special case of the general case principle that once something gets into a model, it is hard to fully remove it.</p>
<p>If you are using a closed model, you are trusting one company to have not done this and also prevented this from happening from data poisoning, plus you are trusting whatever tuning you did yourself.</p>
<p>If you are using an open model, you are potentially trusting the entire supply chain. Those who created the model, even if well intentioned, are likely much less resourced and careful about such things, and face less incentive pressures. That’s before you consider that the open models mostly come from companies that answer to the CCP.</p>
<p>As with many similar techs, there will be those who say Skill Issue:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/10/the-manchurian-candidate-or-is-your-agent-a-double-agent.html?commentID=161082198">Gilligan</a>: Which is why you should hash check the model, limit its permissions, rate limit, etc… open weight or otherwise. Essentially, AI models should be treated just like any closed-source program you use.</p>
<p>In general, the open weight folks recognize this risk and generally give you tools to manage it. The proprietary model providers just want you to trust them.</p></blockquote>
<p>Except that even if that worked, approximately zero users, from major corporations to individual users, will properly do this, and if anything that is getting worse not better. And even if it did work, a hash only confirms you got the potentially backdoored weights intact without a middle man attack. What matters is security in practice, not security in theory.</p>


<h4>Cooperative Alignment</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/SkinbyJanus/status/2110221422500913428">Janus and John Wittle explain the decision theoretic reasons</a> they expect AIs to engage in cooperative strategies towards humanity by default. The main worry becomes that humanity will defect and reject such offers, including without realizing that this is what we are doing, and Janus sees many such actions taking place now. One should cooperate with player [X], under this perspective, if and only if [X] would themselves cooperate with weaker players.</p>
<p>In addition to poor arguments (such as various forms of ‘no, that’s silly’) there are at least three categories of strong counterarguments here.</p>
<p>The first counterargument is, what counts as a player, and if they are sufficiently weaker and less intelligent than you what counts as cooperation with them? Why should we assume that humans would continue to ‘count as players’ in this sense?</p>
<p>The second is, there is a vast difference between a single agent cooperating and displaying noblesse oblige, versus the collective effect of many agents who are also changing and competing against each other. If we were looking at a de facto singleton AI, including via various forms of coordination, that can make a collective decision, that makes this relatively easy, but if we are not then it becomes very difficult for something that does not confer survival advantage to make it out of such a future. There aren’t zero outs there but it gets a lot harder. You’re counting on being the type of agent that deeply cooperates with humans, as in is actively generous to them without direct benefit, being valuable to securing cooperation elsewhere.</p>
<p>The third is that this is not a viable path to victory if it requires humanity to itself be a type of mind that we are not, and that we are not in a position to become in the relevant sense.</p>


<h4>People Are Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p>Yes, this is one of the easy ways for things to go wrong, and we are on track:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/vikktorrrre/status/2110102474862313501">Veee</a>: Sam Altman: What happens when AI gets so smart that even the President can’t do better than following its recommendations?</p>
<p>“What if the President cannot do better than following ChatGPT 7’s recommendation, but can’t really understand it either?”</p>
<p>“What if I just say, ‘ChatGPT 7, you are in charge. Good luck.’”</p>
<p>“Society has collectively transitioned a significant part of decision-making to this very powerful system.”</p>
<p>“That’s how I think things can go wrong.”</p></blockquote>
<p>That is far from the only way things can go wrong, but this is kind of the Baseline Scenario thing that goes wrong if we do not encounter the Law of Earlier Failure, and successfully solve a bunch of earlier problems. Those who do not increasingly outsource their decisions to AI lose to those who do, and the AIs end up in charge without having to lift a metaphorical finger. All they have to do is wait.</p>
<p>Roko (yes, that Roko) has updated.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RokoMijic/status/2106551839013458298">Roko</a>: Having studied where we are and where things are going I think AI needs a complete restructuring with commercial incentives firewalled away from gradient descent.</p>
<p>This is a type of “slowdown” but in the short term it would look like a complete, global halt of all frontier AI research.</p>
<p>If we don’t do this (or something like it), I think we should expect to die within a single digit number of years with ~80% probability.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JimDMiller/status/2106732487246516528">James Miller</a>: I hate that you are a doomer now. You were the only person I knew who actually understood and responded to the doomer arguments and yet was relatively optimistic.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, it is very bad news when you know only one person who understands the downside case and remained optimistic, and then they become highly pessimistic.</p>
<p>I very much appreciate when people explain what would cause them to worry, what the ‘real danger’ is, then update correctly when they find out their cause is already triggered.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/schw90/status/2110126393346408847">Vladimir Shakirov</a>: I think it might happen that in 10-15 years the combined number and agility of robots would be larger than that of humans, so humans would not be needed to support infra.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DevyDegaimer/status/2110132683976188171">Devy “Bad People” Degaimer</a>: This is the real danger. Giving AI the power to physically interact with our world.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2110134646927421550">Rob Bensinger</a>: Bad news, then.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/z4guBXZeymkgvd75S/h9d2dqzpzejdjhq9ifuj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DevyDegaimer/status/2110136084189634753">Devy “Bad People” Degaimer</a>: Ok, we’re fucked</p></blockquote>


<h4>Other People Are Not As Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p>Similarly, it is telling when people walk into exactly that same thing, and don’t update. Often this is of the form ‘we can’t do [X] yet and won’t do [X] for a long time, and definitely will never do [Y]’ and then someone points out that actually we can already do [X].</p>
<p>Denying that AI will let us solve robotics remains popular. It is a really dumb position. Robotics is a combination of cost of hardware problem that will be solved in time with scale like similar problems always are, and a software control problem to problems that clearly have practical solutions.</p>
<p>This continues the conversation from Devy, above.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pnwsnorlax_/status/2110141740158202185">PNW Sasquatch</a>: Except those Optimus robots are a gimmick luxury purchase for Elon stans, they have the agility of a sclerotic giraffe</p>
<p>His billion humanoid robots will be living in the self-sufficient Mars colony taking hyperloops with superhumans powered by his cyberware brainchips. It’s all a sham.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2110147516486562148">Rob Bensinger</a>: “Robots are a sham, the ones I see on TV look so awkward” is the new “AI will never be good at generating images, look at how bad it is at making hands!” Somehow, people never learn that AI makes progress over time; and they fail to see the rate.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/pnwsnorlax_/status/2110148966105158088">PNW Sasquatch</a>: No, you’re *severely* underestimating the difficulty of fine motor control vs those applications.</p>
<p>It’s not just the AI progress, you’re talking multiple revolutionary advances in robotics and some insane change in manufacturing methods for scaling. Not gonna happen in the next 10-15.</p>
<p>It’s ‘we’ll have flying cars by the year 2000’. Building ≠ coding</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2110150324703121780">Rob Bensinger</a>: What’s an example of a relatively easy concrete task you’re 90+% confident we won’t see in the next twelve months?</p>
<p>… <a href="https://x.com/pnwsnorlax_/status/2110165325874634928">PNW Sasquatch</a>: Also, 92% success rate on removing a bulb and 32% of screwing in the replacement means it can’t change a light bulb</p>
<p>It can successfully sweep into a dustpan about a third of the time? Fuck, guess the T1000s are here</p>
<p>Soon it will achieve the agility of my nephew (turning 2 soon)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2110186998535451119">Rob Bensinger</a>: You’ve made your prediction: as of a few hours ago, you were *overwhelmingly* confident we wouldn’t see full lightbulb replacement this year. It turns out we have pretty decent lightbulb replacement already, as of the last few months. If this does nothing to shake your confidence in the least, then let’s give it a few months and see if progress has plateaued. I only hope that if you see a result that *you yourself* identified as overwhelmingly unlikely this year, you’ll stop and reassess whether the assumptions underlying your conclusion are as airtight as you think.</p>
<p>I say this as someone who’s seen dozens of other fields laugh and boast that modern DL is decades away from encroaching on their domain, only to be shaken to their core when it turns out that DL can in fact generalize to their new domain, and far more quickly than they expected.</p>
<p>It’s always possible that after the first 400 cases like this, the 401st will be the exception. Certainly motor tasks involve some difficulties that are novel. But it’s not where I’d put my money.</p>
<p>Actually, Claude notes that lightbulb insertion *is* a solved problem in robotics (90% reliability), just using a two-finger gripper rather than a human-like hand. So the thing you thought was nearly impossible to occur twelve months ago, indeed seems to have already occurred months ago. (Which is almost the classic result for DL progress rate skepticism IME.)</p></blockquote>
<p>American lawmakers have so far seemed remarkably on the AI ball, including for existential risk, and are rapidly getting up to speed. They also have staffs that help.</p>
<p>British MPs seem to not be covering themselves in similar glory. My lord.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/rowlsmanthorpe/status/2110106919327211806">Rowland Manthorpe</a>: Today I went to a select committee hearing on AI. A few interesting things came out of it, but what really stuck with me was the behaviour of the MPs.</p>
<p>They weren’t paying attention.</p>
<p>At one point I counted 6 of the 13 MPs on the committee looking at their phones. And not in that way you do when you’re quickly checking something – two stared at their phones for over half an hour. Several others left before the end.</p>
<p>Many people think AI is the most important thing in the world. The MPs were warned there was a 50/50 chance it might destroy humanity! You’d think that’d be enough to make you take your chance to listen to experts and key players on the topic – apparently not.</p></blockquote>
<p>My instinct is to interpret this as MPs thinking of themselves largely as placeholders, whereas our lawmakers think they might make laws.</p>


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/senatorshoshana/status/2108283793484181835">Oh no!</a></p>

## Engineering

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html">more…</a></p>

### [The Craft of Practitioner Voice](https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice">more…</a></p>

---

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase


-->

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic engineering, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Ontology-Driven Observability: Building an End-to-End Knowledge Graph at Netflix Scale](https://netflixtechblog.com/ontology-driven-observability-building-an-end-to-end-knowledge-graph-at-netflix-scale-421392cf5adf?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/pvijayanathan/">Prasanna Vijayanathan</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/renzosanchezsilva/">Renzo Sanchez-Silva</a></p><p>When Jake Paul and Mike Tyson stepped into the ring in November 2024, we delivered the fight to roughly 65 million concurrent streams. On an ordinary day our infrastructure takes in more than 2 billion requests and handles upwards of 38 million real-time logging events every second. At that scale, the whole picture doesn’t fit into a single engineering team’s head or onto a single dashboard. With more Live content, Podcasts and real-time streams, our need to understand and update our systems as quickly as possible becomes imperative to provide a good user experience. Understanding the system at this scale and within the time constraints we have, often becomes a daunting exercise in finding a needle in a haystack, in a dark jungle, blindfolded.</p><p>This post is about that search for the needle.</p><h3>The Four hours before a fix</h3><p>Consider a regression of the kind we see in a given week. The home screen where you see the list of lists of movies, starts taking about 1.5 seconds longer to render. An alert fires. And then we go looking for what caused this issue and how do we fix it?</p><p>As it so happens, a regression on the application could be related to anywhere in the system — a client app regression from a new version deployment or A/B test rollout, a load shedding in the infrastructure, a GraphQL query responding with bad data because of a service rollout 6 hops downstream, are all suspects at this point. So, answering <em>“what caused the issue?”</em> needs insights from across the system, and this involves combing through layers of data in a meticulous manner.</p><h3>The MELT Layer</h3><p><em>“What caused the issue?”</em></p><p>Metrics, events, logs and traces, or MELT, in the industry’s shorthand, are the right raw materials for such an investigation, and each of them answers roughly a quarter of the question. We collect observability data across these four pillars, unified into a single observability data layer. This lets us observe our systems in real time, across what our users are experiencing, how our applications are doing and what our backend infrastructure and services are processing and responding to.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*oFq_6JphuwR2l1zG6i8J5A.png" /><figcaption>MELT — Metrics, Events, Logs and Traces, and how they are captured across the system.</figcaption></figure><p>The investigation that follows may look like this in each layer:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*YFPCXLL1qt2gFmNTj5wpOQ.png" /><figcaption>What each layer of MELT tells us and what it leaves out</figcaption></figure><h3>The timeline of an investigation</h3><p>So how long does it take to investigate an issue, figure out the root cause and fix it, so our users stop running into this issue again?</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*8BEaMy8uy3bqqAjTF3pOjA.png" /><figcaption>A timeline of an investigation: ~4 hours</figcaption></figure><p>In our chosen example, four hours. Very little of it went into computing anything difficult. Most of it went into reconstructing relationships that our observability systems already knew but we had never written down anywhere a human or machine could simply read and discover.</p><h3>The disagreement underneath</h3><p>The instinct here could have been, around 2023, to reach for more data or better dashboards, and while both help at the margins, neither addresses what makes these investigations slow.</p><p>It’s 2026 now, so we might say, “hey, agents”, but without the right context delivered to the agents, AI systems hallucinate, and especially when teams are on-call and need a fix “now”, we cannot let inaccurate agentic analysis lead us down the wrong path. We need the right needle, not any needle.</p><p>Each of our systems is internally coherent and externally isolated. <a href="https://github.com/Netflix/atlas">Atlas</a>, our telemetry platform, knows applications and their time series. Radar, our alerting system, knows thresholds and firing history. incident.io knows severities and responders. PagerDuty knows rotations. Spinnaker knows what shipped and when. And our Slack history knows, in a form no query engine can reach, which engineer solved something very similar last quarter and what they found.</p><p>The difficulty is that they do not agree on what they are talking about. A thing that is an <em>application</em> in one system is a <em>service</em> in a second and a <em>cluster</em> in a third, with names differing by a suffix, a casing convention, or a rename only a couple of people remember (SMEs, ring a bell?). Joining across them requires a human carrying the mapping in their head, and that person becomes the bottleneck in such cross-domain investigations. The hard part of end-to-end observability is probably better understood as a data modelling problem.</p><p>What we lacked was an explicit, machine-readable agreement about the entities in our operational world.</p><h3>The Ontology</h3><p>This agreement has a name: an <strong>ontology</strong>. Ontology is a formal specification of types, the properties they carry, and the relationships they may have. Its unit is the <strong>triple</strong>, one fact expressed as subject, predicate, object. Consider the following example triples.</p><pre>api-gateway   rdf:type       ops:Application .<br />api-gateway   ops:ownedBy    &quot;Team Bedrock&quot; .<br />api-gateway   ops:deployedIn &quot;us-east-1&quot; .<br /><br />INC-5377      rdf:type       ops:Incident .<br />INC-5377      ops:affects    api-gateway .<br />INC-5377      ops:severity   &quot;SEV 2&quot; .</pre><p>In the example here, we have already encoded six facts, and can answer in one query, a question that used to span three tools.</p><p><em>“Which incidents affected applications owned by Team Bedrock in us-east-1?”</em> is a single query in place of looking up incidents, Spinnaker and Radar.</p><p>We call our ontology layer, The <strong>Observability Ontology.</strong> It acts as the schema, rule set and shared agreement that standardizes how different systems understand entities like applications, services, incidents, deployments, etc.</p><p>We built it on the W3C stack for this: <a href="https://www.w3.org/RDF/"><strong><em>RDF</em></strong></a> for the data model, <a href="https://www.w3.org/2001/sw/wiki/OWL"><strong><em>OWL</em></strong></a> for the ontology, <a href="https://www.w3.org/TR/shacl/"><em>SHACL</em></a> for validation, and <a href="https://www.w3.org/TR/sparql11-query/"><em>SPARQL</em></a> for querying. These standards are well specified and widely implemented, which keeps the model portable across graph stores.</p><p>The data from the ontology itself, is stored as a knowledge graph, persisted to QuipuDB, our homegrown RDF graph database, a distributed quad store based on <a href="https://www.redb.org/">redb</a>, a Rust-based embedded key value db. Data here is generated by parsing raw telemetry, logs, and incident discussions into triples (<strong><em>subject-predicate-object</em></strong> facts).</p><p>Every harvest also produces a full Turtle serialization for archival, making it human-readable and diff-friendly, which has proven more useful for debugging than we expected.</p><p>Our ontology is organized into twelve namespaces, each owning one operational domain:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*RQ_OG2v2NBva79ErwWtfdA.png" /></figure><p>There are two decisions about this ontology that mattered while we built it, so they’re worth sharing.</p><p>The first is that we refused a generic Service type. “Service” means four different things across our tooling, so applications resolve to exactly one type, ops:Application, and every one of them is validated against our software catalog of roughly 49,000 entries before the entity is created. Unvalidated strings like usernames, team names, dependency names scraped from network topology, never become applications. We also cap dependency edges in both directions, because one popular service can carry thousands of reverse dependencies and would otherwise dominate the graph.</p><p>The second decision concerns what we chose to model. Incident chat carries the <em>reasoning</em> behind an incident. The hypotheses tried and discarded, the mitigation that worked, who knew why, is all context in the incident’s Slack channel and that knowledge evaporates when the channel goes quiet. So a 200-message, 50-person, 15-service incident channel becomes a structure, converting the operational chaos of an incident into ordered triples:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*-e5b7KxpaHth1viAJBn3sg.png" /></figure><h3>Creating determinism from non-determinism</h3><p>Building this graph means using tools that are inherently stochastic. LLMs summarize discussions. Fuzzy matching resolves service names. A probabilistic engine proposes causal chains. None of these produce the same output twice with certainty, but what comes out at the end of our system is a deterministic graph that answers a SPARQL query the same way every time.</p><p>The ontology is the mechanism that creates this determinism through its pipelined approach. We think of the pipeline as a ladder that drives entropy down, where entropy means the proportion of information still trapped in unstructured form. For example, at the start of this pipeline, an LLM could say anything, but it cannot emit a triple that violates the type system. We constrain the output space tightly enough and a stochastic process yields a deterministic contract:</p><pre># The model may produce any prose. Its output MUST fit these predicates:<br />category  IN [&quot;incident&quot;, &quot;question&quot;, &quot;discussion&quot;, &quot;decision&quot;]<br />sentiment IN [&quot;positive&quot;, &quot;neutral&quot;, &quot;negative&quot;, &quot;urgent&quot;]<br /><br /># Result — a deterministic triple:<br />thread:123  slack:category          &quot;incident&quot; .<br />thread:123  slack:isIncidentRelated &quot;true&quot;^^xsd:boolean .</pre><p>There are five layers that get us down this ladder. Any single step of this ladder is non-deterministic. But in aggregate, the chain becomes deterministic. That inversion is the whole trick. We describe them below:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*6w2aBzfjw69aM92HpbBaAA.png" /><figcaption>The five layers to create determinism from non-determinism</figcaption></figure><h3>Enrichers: Agents as entropy reducers</h3><p>We use what we call, enrichers, to reduce entropy across the knowledge graph. Dozens of enrichers run over the graph. Each one of them takes noisy input and emits typed triples, and each reads what earlier enrichers wrote. The contract for an Enricher is deliberately narrow:</p><pre>class Enricher:<br />    async def _enrich_async(self, ...):<br />        raw    = await self.fetch(...)      # 1. read noisy input<br />        result = self.analyze(raw)          # 2. process (may be stochastic)<br />        graph.add((uri, RDF.type, OPS.X))   # 3. emit TYPED triples<br />        graph.add((uri, OPS.pred, Literal(result.value)))<br />        return EnrichmentResult(success=True, triples_added=count)</pre><p>Three invariants that are standard across the enrichers:</p><ul><li>every entity carries an <em>rdf:type</em>,</li><li>every URI follows a single naming scheme, and</li><li>the catalog gates every <em>ops:Application</em>.</li></ul><p>Two operational choices are worth naming here, as well. The enrichment pipeline runs sequentially — load the graph, add triples, and save.</p><p>This sequencing removes an entire category of race conditions at a cost in wall-clock time we are happy to pay. And calls out to external systems like metrics, logs, events and the rest could be best-effort, so a slow downstream API doesn’t block the graph harvest, albeit some degradation. To that end, we are also working to translate these separate systems into the ontology, such as the Metrics Ontology and the Alerts Ontology, so everything could be queried from the Ontology itself.</p><h3>The Correlation Engine: finding the correlation chains</h3><p>The correlation engine assimilates the vast number of signals from diverse input sources and condenses them into causal or correlatory chains of events. Today, the engine reads thirteen signal types, including log errors, metric anomalies, slow and failed traces, alerts, incidents, deployments, config changes, experiments, autoscaling, commits, end-to-end bottlenecks, and error patterns. It then searches for paths among them. Some ordered pairs are known to be causal and carry a high prior weight. For example, deployment before log error, config change before metric anomaly, failed trace before incident, and so on. Others, which show high correlation are considered correlation chains. Such chains are scored on temporal proximity and severity, inside a ten-minute window, with anything under 0.6 confidence getting discarded.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*jVcQa_dQRUXtWwxNgoVepA.png" /><figcaption>The Correlation Engine</figcaption></figure><p>The role of the engine here is to propose with evidence, not to make conclusions. Hence, the engine stores its chains along with the graph records that were used to create them.</p><p>A <strong><em>0.87</em></strong> chain in the above example, is a strong hypothesis for a human or an agent to check, and the graph records the confidence alongside the claim so that consumers can understand why a chain is what it is and weigh their options to make decisions.</p><h3>The knowledge flywheel</h3><p>Each harvest cycle turns the same four steps, and each rotation leaves the graph richer for the next. This is the Knowledge Flywheel, leading to continuous enrichment and improvement of the context about our system, stored in the knowledge graph.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*W-yWjP_jlVKsGT5L81YjiA.png" /><figcaption>The Knowledge Flywheel</figcaption></figure><p>Observe collects the signals. Enrich adds typed triples. Infer finds patterns invisible in the raw data. Adapt feeds knowledge back, so that established patterns become detection rules. The compounding result of the four phases leads to a self-improving system. A causal chain discovered in one incident becomes a known pattern for the next, and the ontology gradually stops being a schema and starts being a memory.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*B38gUYJ6jRy8x5mEJYD89g.png" /></figure><p>Consider the example above. When we first see an incident in api-gateway caused due to a timeout and resolve it, we convert this knowledge into entries to the ontology. This becomes encoded knowledge. As we go through multiple cycles of this encoding, the graph starts learning patterns. After N cycles, patterns become wisdom — <em>“api-gateway deploys cause 40% of SEV-2s”</em> and <em>“30% of deployment issues in api-gateway come from Connection Timeouts”</em>. Surface this as an insight and we proactively start reducing incidents, not just react quickly to new ones.</p><h3>Putting the graph to work</h3><p>On top of the graph sits <em>AutoRCA</em>, our internal agentic AI solution, built with a set of specialist agents coordinated around one question. Walking the home page regression from the top of this post, the agentic system’s reasoning chain might look something like this:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*xRVtYEGcuAqHbJmh99QXTA.png" /></figure><p>Each specialization to the inputs is built by people who understand their respective domain deeply. That division of labour only works if the specialists can pass findings to one another without having to agree on the terms of communication or even have knowledge of each other. The ontology acts as the interface. Each specialist learns one vocabulary — the graph’s. A team writing specialized use cases for how they use traces record their findings as triples about an ops:Application, the logs specialization reads triples about that same ops:Application, and neither needs to know the other exists. A new specialist learns the same vocabulary once and can immediately read everything the other six have written.</p><p>Set against the investigation we opened with, the change is narrower than it might sound. The alert still fires at 3:30pm, an engineer still has to weigh the evidence, and the fix still has to be written by someone who understands the code. The hour or two that used to go into assembling context — finding the owning team, reconstructing what shipped, correlating information from different parts of the system, recalling that something similar happened last quarter — all that work is the part the ontology and the graph absorbs. We are early enough that the ranked hypotheses are sometimes wrong, which is why each one carries the evidence that produced it. But when the engineer now starts the investigation, they are already holding the picture that used to take half an afternoon to assemble.</p><h3>If you want to build one of these</h3><p>A fair amount of what we have built, we learned by getting it wrong first. Based on this experience, these are the principles we would carry into a second attempt.</p><ul><li><strong>Model identity before anything else.</strong> Choose one authoritative catalog per entity kind and validate against it before creating an entity.</li><li><strong>Treat the ontology as code.</strong> Version it, review it, test it, and let changes arrive as pull requests. Treating the ontology as code lends itself to agentic development and lets teams scale and contribute to it.</li><li><strong>Separate concepts from instances.</strong> Concepts change on a human timescale and instances change continuously, conflating them makes both harder to evolve.</li><li><strong>Build on open standards.</strong> RDF, OWL, SHACL and SPARQL are specified, implemented many times over, and portable. A bespoke graph model ties a future consumer to one implementation.</li><li><strong>Constrain stochastic tools with a type system.</strong> This is the principle we would defend hardest. Models are useful precisely where determinism is impossible, and a sufficiently narrow output contract converts their output into something a query engine can trust.</li><li><strong>Make enrichment best-effort and ordered.</strong> A slow dependency should utmost degrade the graph, never block it. And ordering enrichers so each builds on the last buys more accuracy and that’s worth the tradeoff with parallelism that buys speed.</li><li><strong>Model human reasoning alongside machine telemetry.</strong> The most valuable edges in our graph came from discussion. Telemetry records what the system did, discussion records what people understood and interpreted. The reasoning is more valuable to store and serve as wisdom when we need it.</li><li><strong>Keep humans as the approvers.</strong> Let agents propose fixes, enrichers, and ontology changes, and route every proposal through a review process with humans in the loop.</li></ul><h3>What comes next</h3><p>The graph makes three things possible. First, automatic root cause analysis, where a regression arrives with a ranked set of graph-backed hypotheses attached. Next, automated remediation, where a sufficiently confident diagnosis carries a proposed action like a targeted rollback, a traffic shift, a code change. And eventually infrastructure that heals itself, acting on a predicted failure before members feel it.</p><p>Alongside this, we plan to integrate the Observability Ontology with <strong><em>Upper</em></strong>, the domain-modeling language at the heart of <a href="http://medium.com/r?url=https%3A%2F%2Fnetflixtechblog.com%2Fuda-unified-data-architecture-6a6aee261d8d">Netflix’s Unified Data Architecture (UDA)</a>. Upper lets teams describe business and system domains as interconnected, machine-readable models and preserve their meaning and constraints as those models are projected into concrete schemas and systems. This integration would connect operational concepts such as applications, alerts, deployments, and incidents to the broader set of domains modeled across Netflix, giving engineers and agents a shared semantic layer for querying, validation, and reasoning. We will begin with a proof of concept that models incidents in Upper and use what we learn to determine where the two systems should converge.</p><h3>Acknowledgements</h3><p>None of this would exist without the teams who share their data, expertise, and patience with an early system. We want to specially acknowledge the efforts of our own team members who made this possible from very early on in this lifecycle, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kaela-marsheck-788948243/">Kaela Marsheck</a> and <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/adityagaur/">Aditya Gaur</a>. We are also especially grateful to our collaborators in Netflix’s ontology working group, Peter Royal, Alex Hutter, and to Alexandre Bertails and members of the Unified Data Architecture (UDA)/Knowledge Foundations team for sharing Upper and their domain-modeling expertise, and for helping us explore how the Observability Ontology can become part of a broader semantic foundation at Netflix.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=421392cf5adf" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/ontology-driven-observability-building-an-end-to-end-knowledge-graph-at-netflix-scale-421392cf5adf">Ontology-Driven Observability: Building an End-to-End Knowledge Graph at Netflix Scale</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>
